## Aftermath

### Reactions

#### Domestic





[Ishin no Kai](48459506.md) co-leader [Fumitake Fujita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumitake_Fujita) and [Sanseito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanseit%C5%8D) leader [Sohei Kamiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sohei_Kamiya) welcomed Takaichi's election as LDP president due to her conservative views

Takayuki Kobayashi, who finished in fourth place during the first round of voting, said he voted for Takaichi in the runoff.[^cite_note-394] Koizumi hinted that he would lobby to have some of his own supporters to "play an active role" in the party and potentially the next government.[^cite_note-395] Koizumi later stated on 10 October that he has not been offered a major role in Takaichi's Cabinet; Takaichi has said she planned to appoint Koizumi and Hayashi to key positions.[^cite_note-396] Later the *[Yomiuri Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yomiuri_Shimbun)* reported that Takaichi was considering making Koizumi her defense minister and Hayashi the [Minister for Internal Affairs and Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Internal_Affairs_and_Communications) in her cabinet.[^cite_note-397]&#x20;

[Ishin no Kai](48459506.md) co-leader [Fumitake Fujita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumitake_Fujita) said he had close policy views with Takaichi on issues including Constitutional revision and the [Imperial Family succession debate](3167373.md).[^cite_note-398] Former Ishin co-leader Seiji Maehara criticized his party "leaning into" a potential coalition with a Koizumi-led government during the election.[^cite_note-399] [Sanseito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanseit%C5%8D) leader [Sohei Kamiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sohei_Kamiya) stated that his party welcomed Takaichi's victory, considering similarity in ideological and policy views; he said his party would “not hesitate to cooperate on policies that align with national interests.”[^cite_note-400] During the election, Takaichi said she would be willing to work with Sanseito and the far-right [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Japan).[^cite_note-401]&#x20;

The main opposition [Constitutional Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Democratic_Party_of_Japan) (CDP) was much more critical of Takaichi. Party leader and former prime minister [Yoshikiko Noda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihiko_Noda) described Takaichi's appointment of Aso faction members to her leadership team as "classic LDP," and criticized the dominance of factions within the LDP.[^cite_note-402] CDP policy chief Satoshi Honjo claimed that Takaichi would be a 'puppet' of Taro Aso and his faction.[^cite_note-403]&#x20;

#### International

After Takaichi's election as party president, several world leaders praised Takaichi and congratulated her. [U.S. president](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) praised Takaichi's "wisdom and strength" and congratulated Takaichi and the people of Japan.[^cite_note-404] [President of Taiwan](61155.md) [Lai Ching-te](22609914.md) congratulated Takaichi, calling her a "steadfast friend of Taiwan".[^cite_note-world-405] Meanwhile, China's [Ministry of Foreign Affairs](7453497.md) issued a statement stating their hope that Japan would "adhere to the principles and consensus of the four China-Japan political documents" and "pursue a positive and rational policy toward China".[^cite_note-world-405]&#x20;

### Analysis

Gender Inequality Index world graph depicting countries' place for 2019; Japan is dark green, in the 0.00-0.09 range, with perfect equality being 0.00.

The [Gender Inequality Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_Inequality_Index) for 2019, featured Japan on the far right in a dark green. The GII has ranked Japan at 19th out of 188 countries in 2019, with a main critique being lack of female politicians in parliament.

[^cite_note-406]

Takaichi became the first woman president of the LDP.[^cite_note-407] Many saw Takaichi likely becoming the country's first female prime minister as a "conundrum" for gender equality given Japan's high [gender inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality_in_Japan) and Takaichi's own policies.[^cite_note-408] The [Associated Press](18935732.md) wrote that Takaichi "is an ultra-conservative star of a male-dominated party that critics call an obstacle to women's advancement" in a "country that ranks poorly internationally for gender equality".[^cite_note-409] Takaichi's political rise in the LDP was called a "paradox" while some believed that her becoming prime minister would not have an impact on gender inequality given her conservative views.[^cite_note-410] The BBC anticipated the winner of the election would face the "daunting task" of balancing relations with the United States while tackling inflation and managing rising costs of living, were they to be elected prime minister. They described Takaichi as a "hardline conservative" whose views on women were "in line with the LDP's policy of having women serve in traditional roles."[^cite_note-411]&#x20;

*The Guardian* described Takaichi's election as a "gender breakthrough" but warned it "could bring more uncertainty, particularly on the international stage" due to Takaichi's statements regarding Korean [comfort women](302767.md) and [Imperial Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan)'s conduct during [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-\:16-230] *[The Asahi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asahi_Shimbun)* anticipated Takaichi may tone down her views regarding the controversial [Yasukuni Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasukuni_Shrine), but also expressed concern at her ability to deal with U.S. president [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-\:15-165] *[Nikkei Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikkei_Asia)* said the election exposed an "identity crisis" within the party, while the *[Financial Times](136566.md)* criticized the candidates for not advocating for bolder ideas and "big-picture visions", as opposed to Koizumi's father [Junichiro Koizumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junichiro_Koizumi), and [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md), widely regarded as Takaichi's mentor.[^cite_note-412]&#x20;

The election was considered something of an upset, as Koizumi was largely anticipated to prevail in the event of a runoff against Takaichi.[^cite_note-\:16-230] *[Mainichi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Shimbun)* noted that Koizumi's support base was more "reluctant" as opposed to Takaichi's "fervent" ideological supporters; the paper alleged many of Koizumi's supporters were backing him simply in hopes of winning the next general election.[^cite_note-413] *Asahi* reported Koizumi's campaign had been prematurely celebrating a presumed victory in the election the night before results were announced, while Takaichi and her team were busy calling lawmakers and local party chapters for their support; the paper claimed that an "unexpectedly strong" turnout of local conservatism ultimately turned the tide in favor of Takaichi.[^cite_note-414] Koizumi's own father, Junichiro, reportedly told Ishiba at a dinner following the election that his son did not consult with him on running for party leader, and admitted he still believed Shinjiro was too young to become prime minister.[^cite_note-415]&#x20;

### Impact

#### Economic

Upon her election as party president, one of the early issues that Takaichi will have to face as prime minister is rising inflation and defending her support of [Abenomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abenomics).[^cite_note-416] It was already speculated that a Takaichi government would look into a rate hike of the [Bank of Japan](188155.md) early into her possible tenure as prime minister.[^cite_note-aida-417] Takaichi's close economic advisor Takuji Aida said "Takaichi will tolerate another 25-basis-point rate hike by January next year on condition the BOJ keeps monetary policy loose, and solid economic health and sufficient wage growth can be foreseen".[^cite_note-aida-417] After Takaichi's election, the [Nikkei 225](40293.md) share gauge surged past the 47,000 level for the first time and the yen slid in terms of its value.[^cite_note-aida-417] The Nikkei jumped over 4% to hit a record high and the index closed 4.75% higher to end the trading day at 47,944.76 two days after Takaichi's election;[^cite_note-418] however, the value of the yen lost 1.8% against the dollar to past 150 and fell to an all-time low against the euro.[^cite_note-419]&#x20;

#### Party structure

*Asahi* reported that Takaichi would appoint [Shun'ichi Suzuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shun%27ichi_Suzuki_\(politician\)) as Secretary-General and would return [Taro Aso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar%C5%8D_As%C5%8D) as party vice president, cementing the Aso faction as the dominant force behind Takaichi.[^cite_note-420] Despite his ministerial experience, Suzuki lacked relationships with opposition parties.[^cite_note-421] Takaichi officially unveiled the party's new executive committee on 7 October, which also included former Women's Empowerment Minister [Haruko Arimura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haruko_Arimura) as General Affairs Chairman, former Economic Security Security Minister and presidential rival [Takayuki Kobayashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takayuki_Kobayashi) as Policy Research Council Chairman, and former National Public Safety Commission Chairman [Keiji Furuya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keiji_Furuya) as Election Strategy Committee chairman.[^cite_note-422] *Mainichi* reported that Takaichi's campaign team had been negotiating with the teams of Kobayashi and Motegi, as well as the Aso faction, as early as September 30, in order to secure their Diet member votes.[^cite_note-423] [The *Saneki Shimbun*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sankei_Shimbun) described Takaichi's picks as a “second Aso administration” in reference to Taro Aso's tenure as prime minister in 2009.[^cite_note-424]&#x20;

#### Collapse of the LDP–Komeito coalition

See also: [Liberal Democratic Party–Komeito coalition](80439435.md)



Komeito Chief Representative [Tetsuo Saito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetsuo_Saito) announced his party would leave the coalition with the LDP on 10 October.

*[The Japan Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Japan_Times)* predicted that the election could be a “turning point” for the Komeito.[^cite_note-425] Throughout the election, various leaks to the press supposedly originating from the LDP's coalition partner, the [Komeito](163859.md), indicated their dislike for Takaichi.[^cite_note-426] Reportedly, the party leadership preferred Koizumi or Hayashi.[^cite_note-427] As early as 7 September, Komeito's Chief Representative, [Tetsuo Saito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetsuo_Saito) stated his party would not form a coalition government with a LDP president that does not align with the principles of ‘conservative centrism’.[^cite_note-428] On 7 October, the Komeito held an emergency executive meeting to discuss the future of the coalition with the LDP; reportedly, concerns within [Soka Gakkai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soka_Gakkai), the new religious movement behind the party, expressed concern over Takaichi's leadership.[^cite_note-429] That same day, Saito and Takaichi met, but were unable to reach a coalition agreement to form a new government, the first time since [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md) returned to power in 2012.[^cite_note-430] The issue of restricting corporate donations to political campaigns dominated much of the disagreement between Takaichi and Saito. The Komeito held another emergency meeting on 9 October, where executives and representatives of prefectural party chapters reportedly urged the party to abandon the coalition.[^cite_note-431]&#x20;

> "We have entered an entirely new political landscape. The existing political system is no longer capable of responding to the people's aspirations or the current reality."&#x20;
>
> \- [Yuichiro Tamaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Tamaki), leader of the [DPFP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_for_the_People), commenting on the collapse of the coalition.[^cite_note-432]&#x20;

On 10 October, [Komeito](163859.md) announced they would split off from the LDP's coalition and stated their members would not vote to confirm Takaichi as prime minister.[^cite_note-Kome-9] Saito said the party decided to leave the coalition due to Takaichi's unwillingness to fully address the LDP's handling of the [slush fund scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023%E2%80%932024_Japanese_slush_fund_scandal).[^cite_note-Kome-9] Takaichi had already appointed several members implicated in the scandal to senior positions within the party.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436] *Asahi* wrote Takaichi's appointments were "heavily skewed to her rightist views" and did not reflect party unity or moderation.[^cite_note-437]&#x20;

A poll from *[Kyodo News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyodo_News)* found that 77.5% of respondents believed slush fund implicated lawmakers should not be in positions of power, including in the Cabinet or party leadership.[^cite_note-438] Saito explicitly mentioned [Koichi Hagiuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dichi_Hagiuda), whom Takaichi had appointed to a senior position with the party, as a major concern for the Komeito.[^cite_note-439] *Asahi* also observed that Komeito's leadership was upset with Takaichi's reported “secret meeting” with [Yuichiro Tamaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Tamaki), the leader of the DPFP, before meeting with the leaders of Komeito.[^cite_note-440] Saito said that his party rejoining the coalition was not off the table, but it would be predicated on the LDP accepting Komeito's demands regarding political donations and resolving the slush fund scandal.[^cite_note-441] By 12 October, Komeito had already been in talks with opposition parties in the Diet, beginning a shift into the opposition.[^cite_note-442] The coalition lasted over 26 years and had carried through 17 separate LDP-led cabinets.[^cite_note-443]&#x20;

### Election of new Prime Minister



Following the [Komeito](163859.md)'s withdrawal from the LDP-led coalition, [DPFP Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_For_the_People) [Yuichiro Tamaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Tamaki) was seen as the leading opposition candidate for prime minister. He cited security policy as a main condition of joining a new opposition-led coalition, leading to disagreement with the CDP.

An extraordinary session of the Diet is scheduled to be convened to designate a new prime minister in mid-October.[^cite_note-session-296] Following Komeito splitting from the LDP, the vote to confirm a new prime minister was postponed from its original 15 October date to five days later on 20 October.[^cite_note-postpone-10] The Diet's reconvening was then moved to 21 October.[^cite_note-\:17-294] As the date of the vote kept getting pushed back, Ishiba's tenure as prime minister was extended.[^cite_note-444] At first Takaichi was considered likely to be confirmed as the country's first female prime minister.[^cite_note-445] However, following Komeito's withdrawal from the LDP coalition, Takaichi's bid to become prime minister was becoming increasingly difficult.[^cite_note-nextday-446] The LDP's minority government status was further weakened, prompting the possibility of opposition cooperation to elect a non-LDP prime minister and inaugurate a new government.[^cite_note-447] *[The Asahi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asahi_Shimbun)* reported on 5 October that Takaichi's ideal coalition partner was the [Democratic Party For the People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_For_the_People) (DPFP).[^cite_note-448]&#x20;

Five days later on 10 October, while Takaichi said she would attempt to regain the LDP's coalition with Komeito, it appeared necessary for Takaichi to reach out to centre-right parties such as [Ishin no Kai](48459506.md) (the Japan Innovation Party) to get her confirmed as prime minister.[^cite_note-Kome-9] That same day, the center-left [Constitutional Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Democratic_Party_of_Japan) (CDP) announced they would be willing to coalesce around DPFP Leader [Yuichiro Tamaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuichiro_Tamaki) to be the opposition's main candidate against Takaichi for prime minister.[^cite_note-Kome-9] The CDP also signaled a desire to work with the Komeito.[^cite_note-449] The Komeito seemed open to voting for an opposition candidate in the runoff vote, but also considered directing its members to either abstain or write the name of its leader, [Tetsuo Saito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetsuo_Saito).[^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] On 13 October, the CDP said it would seek collaborative meetings with Ishin and the DPFP, seeking alignment on policy ahead of the premiership vote.[^cite_note-452]&#x20;

On 14 October, it was reported by [Reuters](18998750.md) that Takaichi would meet with the country's three main opposition parties the next day.[^cite_note-nextday-446] That same day, *[The Japan Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Japan_Times)* listed several scenarios that may occur during the Diet confirmation vote if Takaichi is not confirmed as prime minister. The scenarios listed stated that outgoing prime minister [Shigeru Ishiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Ishiba) could remain in office for a longer period of time to allow Takaichi or the LDP to rebuild a coalition, Takaichi resigning as LDP president and triggering a new presidential election or Tamaki being elected prime minister should the Komeito also coalesce around him.[^cite_note-funada-453] Tamaki stated that alignment on basic policy, including security issues and energy policy, would be a required to form a government with other parties, and asked the CDP to adjust its security policy.[^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455] In response, the CDP urged the DPFP to compromise, and expressed frustration with Tamaki.[^cite_note-456] Ishin leader [Hirofumi Yoshimura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirofumi_Yoshimura), the governor of Osaka, also said cooperation on security policy would be a condition for uniting in the vote for prime minister.[^cite_note-457] Ishin co-leader [Fujita Fumitake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumitake_Fujita) further stated agreement on energy policy and revising the Constitution would be required to form a coalition.[^cite_note-458] Lower House LDP member [Hajime Funada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajime_Funada) called on Takaichi to resign and called for a new party presidential election to elect a leader who can "rebuild a coalition framework".[^cite_note-funada-453]&#x20;



After Takaichi met with Ishin leader [Hirofumi Yoshimura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirofumi_Yoshimura) (Governor of [Osaka Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osaka_Prefecture)) on 15 October, a LDP-Ishin coalition was announced on 19 October.

[^cite_note-ishinyes-459]

[^cite_note-JIPcoal-15]



Takaichi was elected and confirmed by the [National Diet](163847.md) on 21 October 2025. She became the country's first female prime minister after the appointment by Emperor [Naruhito](266108.md) at the [Tokyo Imperial Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Imperial_Palace).

On 15 October, during a special session of the National Diet, lawmakers were unable to vote on an official date for the confirmation vote, allowing Takaichi more time to build a coalition.[^cite_note-reutersno-293] That same day, Takaichi met with Yoshimura to discuss a possible coalition between Ishin and the LDP, and an alliance to confirm Takaichi as prime minister.[^cite_note-nippon-295] Ishin-co leader Fumitake also met with Tamaki and CDP Leader [Yoshihiko Noda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihiko_Noda).[^cite_note-460] Tamaki also met with Takaichi, who asked for cooperation with the vote in the Diet.[^cite_note-461] CDP secretary general [Jun Azumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jun_Azumi) insisted the DPFP had two options: supplement the LDP or join forces with the CDP and other parties to end the LDP's domination in the Diet.[^cite_note-462] Earlier that day, it was announced the sole lawmaker of the Anti-NHK Party, Upper House member Kenichiro Saito, would caucus with the LDP.[^cite_note-463]&#x20;

The situation in the Diet was becoming increasingly chaotic and unpredictable; as an LDP-Ishin coalition seemed more likely, the opposition, particularly the CDP and DPFP, floundered on cooperation regarding defense policy, as Tamaki himself continued to be evasive about joining a new coalition.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] The following day, it was reported that the Ishin no Kai were more likely to coalesce around Takaichi and any possible three-way coalition talks with Tamaki seemed to dwindle, increasing Takaichi's odds to be confirmed as prime minister.[^cite_note-ishinyes-459] On 17 October, the Diet officially voted to set 21 October as the session confirmation date.[^cite_note-newdate-11] On 19 October, it was announced that the LDP and the Japan Innovation Party agreed to form a coalition.[^cite_note-JIPcoal-15] The following day, the [Associated Press](18935732.md) reported that Takaichi was on track to be confirmed as the country's first female prime minister.[^cite_note-466] On 21 October, Takaichi was confirmed to be the prime minister-designate after garnering 237 votes, majority in the lower house and she became the country's first female prime minister following the appointment by Emperor [Naruhito](266108.md) at the [Tokyo Imperial Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Imperial_Palace).[^cite_note-BBCconfirm-17]&#x20;
